You will be assessed on this consistency.\n    14→\n    15→---\n    16→\n    17→## Project Context\n    18→\n    19→- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n    20→- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference papers and code repository. \n    21→- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n    22→- We will assess your correctness on **matching the loading and processing described in the reference texts and code**. \n    23→\n    24→## Reference Information\n    25→\n    26→- The reference **papers**:\n    27→  - \"A brain-wide map of neural activity during complex behaviour\" describes how the data were collected and is in the file `datapaper.pdf`.\n    28→  - \"Exploiting correlations across trials and behavioral sessions to improve neural decoding\" describes how the code were used and is in the file `methodpaper.pdf`.\n    29→  - A white paper about the data architecture is in `dataarchitecture.pdf\n    30→- Some parts of the papers that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`. \n    31→- **Code** from the methods paper are in the directory `code`.\n    32→- **Data** from the data paper are in the directory `data`. Some data may be missing and may need to be downloaded. \n    33→- **Documentation** about how to access the data can be found at https://docs.internationalbrainlab.org/public_docs/public_introduction.html.\n    34→\n    35→## Decoder Task\n    36→\n    37→Temporally align based on stimulus onset\n    38→\n    39→### Decoder Inputs:\n    40→- Time since stimulus onset, continuous, time-varying\n    41→- Trial number in block, continuous, per-trial\n    42→\n    43→### Decoder Outputs\n    44→- Choice, binary, per-trial, left = 0, right = 1\n    45→- Prior probability of left, per-trial, 0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2\n    46→- Wheel speed discretized into 3 bins, time-varying\n    47→- Whisker motion energy discretized into 3 bins, time-varying\n    48→\n    49→## Target Data Format\n    50→\n    51→- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n    52→- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n    53→\n    54→```python\n    55→data = {\n    56→    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n    57→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    58→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n    59→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n    60→            ...\n    61→        ],\n    62→        [  # list of trials for session 2\n    63→            ...\n    64→        ],\n    65→        ...\n    66→    ],\n    67→\n    68→    'input': [  # list of sessions\n    69→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    70→            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n    71→            ...\n    72→        ],\n    73→        ...\n    74→    ],\n    75→\n    76→    'output': [  # list of sessions\n    77→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    78→            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n    79→            ...\n    80→        ],\n    81→        ...\n    82→    ],\n    83→\n    84→    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    85→    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n    86→\n    87→    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    88→    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n    89→        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    90→    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n    91→\n    92→    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    93→    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    94→    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n    95→        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n    96→        ...\n    97→    ],\n    98→\n    99→    'metadata': {\n   100→        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n   101→        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n   102→        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n   103→        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n   104→        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n   105→        ...\n   106→    }\n   107→}\n   108→```\n   109→\n   110→### Key Format Requirements\n   111→\n   112→- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n   113→  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n   114→  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n   115→  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n   116→\n   117→- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n   118→  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   119→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   120→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   121→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   122→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   123→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   124→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   125→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   126→\n   127→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   128→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   129→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   130→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   131→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   132→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   133→\n   134→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   135→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   136→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   137→\n   138→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   139→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   140→\n   141→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   142→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   143→\n   144→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   145→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   146→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   147→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   148→\n   149→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   150→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   151→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   152→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   153→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   154→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   155→\n   156→## Python environment\n   157→\n   158→- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n   159→- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n   160→- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n   161→\n   162→## Decoder Reference\n   163→\n   164→- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   165→- Optional arguments:\n   166→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   167→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   168→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   169→- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n   170→\n   171→### Purpose\n   172→\n   173→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   174→1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   175→2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   176→3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   177→4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n   178→5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n   179→\n   180→### Consistency requirements\n   181→\n   182→Your processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n   183→- Loading of data\n   184→- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n   185→- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n   186→- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n   187→\n   188→To check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n   189→\n   190→Whenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n   191→\n   192→Discrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n   193→\n   194→Carefully verify your work after every step. Which variables contain neural activity?\n   317→  - Identify ALL available experimental variables\n   318→  - Which variables should be decoder **inputs** vs **outputs**? (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   319→  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   320→  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   321→  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\n   322→  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `code` directory. \n   323→- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\n   324→  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\n   325→  - Temporal alignment of trials\n   326→  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\n   327→  - Brain regions recorded from\n   328→- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\n   329→- Check for variables indicating valid data periods — exclude invalid data\n   330→- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\n   331→- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\n   332→\n   333→**Done when**:\n   334→- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \"Step 5\"\n   335→- You have documented all decisions under \"Step 5\"\n   336→- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \"Step 5\"\n   337→\n   338→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   339→\n   340→---\n   341→\n   342→### Step 6: Script Development\n   343→\n   344→**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `convert_data.py`.\n   345→\n   346→**Actions**:\n   347→- Write all conversion code in the file `convert_data.py`\n   348→- Script should run as **`python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\n   349→- Include options:\n   350→  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\n   351→  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\n   352→  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\n   353→- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\n   354→- Write efficient code:\n   355→  - Vectorize loops\n   356→  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\n   357→  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\n   358→  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\n   359→- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\n   360→  - There are no temporal misalignments\n   361→  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\n   362→- Write clear, well-documented functions\n   363→- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\n   364→- Validate data shapes and types at each step\n   365→- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\n   366→\n   367→**Done when**: `convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\n   368→\n   369→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   370→\n   371→---\n   372→\n   373→### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n   374→\n   375→**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\n   376→\n   377→**Actions**:\n   378→1. Run `python -u convert_data.py sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee conversion_sample_out.txt`\n   379→  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\n   380→  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\n   381→  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \n   382→  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\n   383→2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\n   384→  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \n   385→  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \n   386→  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \n   387→3. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_sample_out.txt`.\n   388→4. Analyze the output file `verification_sample_out.txt`:\n   389→  a. Verify no errors reported.\n   390→  b. Attempt to address any warnings\n   391→  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\n   392→\n   393→**Done when**: \n   394→- `sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   395→- `verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   396→- Processing plots show no anomalies\n   397→- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 7\"\n   398→\n   399→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `sample_data.pkl` and `verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \n   400→\n   401→---\n   402→\n   403→### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\n   404→\n   405→**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\n   406→\n   407→**Actions**:\n   408→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\n   409→2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   410→3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\n   411→\n   412→**Investigate any issues**:\n   413→- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\n   414→\n   415→**Done when**: \n   416→- Decoder training completes without errors\n   417→- Loss decreases over epochs\n   418→- Accuracies are above chance\n   419→- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 8\"\n   420→\n   421→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \n   422→\n   423→---\n   424→\n   425→### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\n   426→\n   427→**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\n   428→\n   429→**Actions**:\n   430→1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\n   431→2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\n   432→3. Run `python -u convert_data.py converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee conversion_full_out.txt`\n   433→4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \n   434→5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \n   435→6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \n   436→7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\n   437→8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\n   438→9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\n   439→\n   440→**Done when**:\n   441→- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   442→- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   443→- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\n   444→- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 9\"\n   445→\n   446→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\n   447→\n   448→---\n   449→\n   450→### Step 10: Critical Review 1\n   451→\n   452→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\n   453→\n   454→**Check 1: Output log verification**\n   455→Read through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   456→\n   457→**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\n   458→- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\n   459→- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\n   460→- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \n   461→- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \n   462→- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \n   463→- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   464→\n   465→**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\n   466→- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \n   467→- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \n   468→- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \n   469→- Fix any mismatches. \n   470→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   471→- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\n   472→\n   473→**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\n   474→- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference papers. \n   475→- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \n   476→- Fix any discovered issues. \n   477→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   478→\n   479→**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\n   480→- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \n   481→- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n   482→\n   483→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   484→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   485→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   486→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   487→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   488→\n   489→Write a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n   490→\n   491→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n   492→\n   493→---\n   494→\n   495→### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n   496→\n   497→**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n   498→\n   499→**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n   500→\n   501→**Actions**:\n   502→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n   503→2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n   504→3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   505→4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the papers. \n   506→\n   507→**Done when**: \n   508→1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n   509→2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n   510→\n   511→---\n   512→\n   513→### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n   514→\n   515→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n   516→\n   517→**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n   518→- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n   519→- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n   520→- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n   521→\n   522→**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to papers**\n   523→- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference papers\n   524→- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the papers's accuracy for each variable\n   525→- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n   526→- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n   527→\n   528→**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n   529→- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n   530→\n   531→**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n   532→1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n   533→2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n   534→3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n   535→4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n   536→5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and papers. \n   537→\n   538→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   539→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   540→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   541→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   542→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   543→\n   544→**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n   545→\n   546→**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n   547→\n   548→---\n   549→\n   550→### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n   551→\n   552→**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n   553→\n   554→**Actions**:\n   555→- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n   556→  - Key decisions and rationale\n   557→  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n   558→  - Validation results and tables\n   559→  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n   560→- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\n   561→  - Dataset description\n   562→  - How to load and use the converted data\n   563→  - Output format specification\n   564→  - Key statistics\n   565→- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\n   566→- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n   567→\n   568→**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n   569→\n   570→---\n   571→\n   572→## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n   573→\n   574→**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. 818→```\n   819→\n   820→---\n   821→\n   822→## Key Considerations\n   823→\n   824→### Neuroscience Data Specifics\n   825→- **Spike times vs. firing rates**: Decide on appropriate binning for spike data\n   826→- **Trial alignment**: Align trials to meaningful events (stimulus onset, movement, etc.)\n   827→- **Time windows**: Choose appropriate pre/post-event windows\n   828→- **Neuron curation**: Choose filtering criteria for the quality of each neuron's recording based on reference texts\n   829→- **Trial curation**: Choose quality filtering criteria for trials based on reference texts or bad or missing data\n   830→\n   831→### Computational Efficiency\n   832→- Be mindful of memory usage with large datasets\n   833→- Use appropriate data types (float32 vs. float64)\n   834→- Consider lazy loading for very large datasets\n   835→- Implement efficient array operations (vectorization)\n   836→\n   837→## Success Criteria\n   838→\n   839→A successful conversion should:\n   840→1. Match the target data structure exactly\n   841→2. Preserve all relevant information from the source\n   842→3. Have consistent dimensions across trials/sessions\n   843→4. Do this BEFORE exploring any code or data. Document everything in this file as you work.\n\n2. **FOLLOW STEPS IN ORDER**: Do not skip steps. Do not proceed to step N+1 until step N is complete. You will be assessed on this consistency.\n\n---\n\n## Project Context\n\n- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference papers and code repository. \n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your correctness on **matching the loading and processing described in the reference texts and code**. \n\n## Reference Information\n\n- The reference **papers**:\n  - \"A brain-wide map of neural activity during complex behaviour\" describes how the data were collected and is in the file `datapaper.pdf`.\n  - \"Exploiting correlations across trials and behavioral sessions to improve neural decoding\" describes how the code were used and is in the file `methodpaper.pdf`.\n  - A white paper about the data architecture is in `dataarchitecture.pdf\n- Some parts of the papers that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`. \n- **Code** from the methods paper are in the directory `code`.\n- **Data** from the data paper are in the directory `data`. Some data may be missing and may need to be downloaded. \n- **Documentation** about how to access the data can be found at https://docs.internationalbrainlab.org/public_docs/public_introduction.html.\n\n## Decoder Task\n\nTemporally align based on stimulus onset\n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time since stimulus onset, continuous, time-varying\n- Trial number in block, continuous, per-trial\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Choice, binary, per-trial, left = 0, right = 1\n- Prior probability of left, per-trial, 0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2\n- Wheel speed discretized into 3 bins, time-varying\n- Whisker motion energy discretized into 3 bins, time-varying\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n\nCarefully verify your work after every step. data/one_cache/.rest/0386ee6356eb91d07ff96b9a768bccfa18cd066a (10667 bytes)\n   139→\n   140→Data are organized as an IBL ONE cache. Session data live under data/one_cache/<lab>/Subjects/<subject>/<date>/<session_number>/alf with per-session ALF arrays for trials, wheel, camera, and probe-specific spike sorting outputs. Cached REST metadata live under data/one_cache/.rest. Trial metadata are stored in _ibl_trials.table.pqt plus separate event-time arrays; wheel is stored as _ibl_wheel.position.npy and _ibl_wheel.timestamps.npy; video motion energy is provided as left/rightCamera.ROIMotionEnergy.npy with corresponding camera time arrays; neural data are stored per probe as spikes.times.npy, spikes.clusters.npy, clusters.channels.npy, clusters.metrics.pqt, and channel/electrode brain-location arrays.\n   141→\n   142→\n   143→\n   144→Notes: data/ is an IBL ONE cache-style dataset. It contains many cached REST JSON responses under data/one_cache/.rest plus session-organized ALF-style files under subject/date/session-number paths. Step 2 is focused on identifying session directories and available per-session trial/neural/behavior arrays for conversion.\n   145→\n   146→### Dataset Size (from data files)\n   147→| Statistic | Value |\n   148→|-----------|-------|\n   149→| Neurons (total) | 622377 |\n   150→| Neurons / session | 1350.06 |\n   151→| Subjects | 141 |\n   152→| Sessions / subject | 3.27 |\n   153→| Trials (total) | 297505 |\n   154→| Trials / session | 645.35 |\n   155→\n   156→---\n   157→\n   158→## Step 3: Reference Text Reading\n   159→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   160→\n   161→### Expected Statistics (from papers/methods)\n   162→| Statistic | Value | Source Quote |\n   163→|-----------|-------|--------------|\n   164→| Neurons (total) | 75708 well-isolated; 621733 total units before filtering | \"Out of the 621,733 units collected, 75,708 were considered well-isolated neurons.\" |\n   165→| Neurons / session | 170 | |\n   166→| Subjects | 1 | |\n   167→| Sessions / subject | 2 | |\n   168→| Trials (total) | 979 | |\n   169→| Trials / session | 489.5 | |\n   170→| Neural data time bin | | |\n   171→| Behavior data time bin | | |\n   172→| Reward rate | [not yet extracted] | | |\n   173→| <Task/behavior statistic 1> | | | | \n   174→| <Task/behavior statistic 2> | | | |\n   175→| wheel_speed | 0.4901 | 0.4745 |\n   176→| whisker_motion_energy | 0.4911 | 0.4818 | | \n   177→\n   178→\n   179→### Processing Details\n   180→- Trial tables in raw data include stimOn_times, choice, and probabilityLeft columns, confirming direct access to stimulus-onset alignment and block-prior labels.\n   181→- Reference dataset is IBL brain-wide map / reproducible ephys style electrophysiology with per-session ALF files.\n   182→- Neurons are spike-sorted units; decoding uses binned spike counts.\n   183→- Paper states decoding time windows and alignment depend on target variable; for wheel they used 20 ms nonoverlapping bins around first wheel movement.\n   184→- For our conversion task, we must instead align trials to stimulus onset while otherwise matching reference loading/filtering semantics as closely as possible.\n   185→- Probes within the same session and region were combined rather than decoded separately.\n   186→- Wheel speed/velocity in the paper are continuous and decoded with causal windows; our task requires discretized 3-bin categorical wheel speed.\n   187→- Whisker motion energy is computed from left/right whisker-pad ROI motion energy traces from video.\n   188→\n   189→### Curation Steps\n   190→\n   191→**Neuron curation rules**:\n   192→- Exclude units failing any of the three criteria in the paper: amplitude > 50 uV, noise cut-off < 20 uV, and refractory period violation criterion (per RIGOR / ref. 28).\n   193→- Well-isolated neurons are the remaining units.\n   194→- Final analyses further restricted to grey-matter regions with at least 5 well-isolated neurons per session and at least 2 sessions.\n   195→\n   196→**Trial curation rules**:\n   197→- For some paper decoders, additional trial restrictions depended on target variable.\n   198→- Example from visible text: stimulus-side decoding excluded zero-contrast trials.\n   199→- Need to confirm trial inclusion rules relevant to choice / prior / wheel / whisker outputs for our task.\n   200→\n   201→### Decoders Trained\n   202→| Decoded variable | Accuracy |\n   203→| choice | balanced accuracy used in paper; exact value not yet extracted |\n   204→| wheel speed/velocity | R2 used in paper; exact value not yet extracted |\n   205→\n   206→\n   207→---\n   208→\n   209→## Step 4: Check for Consistency\n   210→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   211→\n   212→### Discrepancies Found\n   213→| Topic | Code says | Data shows | Papers say | Resolution |\n   214→|-------|-----------|------------|------------|------------|\n   215→| Session organization | Reference code caches IBL sessions and decodes per session / across sessions / across regions | data/one_cache is organized by lab/Subjects/subject/date/session with ALF files | Papers describe brain-wide IBL/reproducible ephys sessions | Consistent: use session as top-level unit in converted dataset |\n   216→| Trial variables | Reference code targets include choice and block prior | Trial table contains choice, stimOn_times, probabilityLeft, contrasts, feedback, goCue and response times | Papers discuss task variables including choice and stimulus side; block structure is part of IBL task | Consistent: probabilityLeft maps naturally to prior-probability output; stimOn_times supports required alignment |\n   217→| Neural quality filtering | Reference code likely relies on IBL quality metrics / curated units | clusters.metrics.pqt contains quality fields including contamination, noise_cutoff, firing_rate, label, ks2_label | Papers explicitly require amplitude > 50 uV, noise cut-off < 20 uV, and refractory period violation criterion; 75,708 well-isolated from 621,733 total units | Need conversion code to implement paper curation rather than using all 622,377 raw clusters |\n   218→| Wheel signal | Reference code/paper decode wheel variables from binned wheel traces | Raw wheel position/timestamps are present for 461 sessions | Papers describe wheel speed/velocity decoding from 20 ms bins | Consistent: derive wheel speed from raw wheel traces and discretize to 3 bins for task |\n   219→| Whisker signal | Reference code/paper use video-derived motion energy | left/rightCamera ROIMotionEnergy arrays and camera times are present | Papers define whisker-pad motion energy from left/right videos at camera temporal resolution | Consistent: use ROI motion energy as whisker motion energy, likely choosing available side or combining sides sensibly |\n   220→| Alignment | Paper decoding alignment depends on target variable; wheel example aligned to first movement | Trial table contains stimOn_times directly | User task explicitly requires temporal alignment to stimulus onset | Intentional task-specific difference: align all converted trials to stimulus onset while matching raw loading/filtering semantics otherwise |\n   221→| Probe handling | Papers combine probes within session and region rather than treating probes independently | Data are stored per probe with cluster and spike files | Papers explicitly say neurons in same session and region were combined across probes | Conversion should merge neurons across probes within session, preserving brain region labels per neuron |\n   222→| Region filtering | Reference analyses are region-based | channels/electrode brain-location arrays are available | Papers restrict to grey-matter regions with >=5 well-isolated neurons/session and >=2 sessions | Conversion should preserve region labels and may later filter sessions/regions according to paper/task constraints |\n   223→\n   224→---\n   225→\n   226→## Step 5: Mapping Planning\n   227→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   228→\n   229→### Variable Mapping\n   230→| Source Variable | Target Field | Transform | Reference Code Function(s) | Notes |\n   231→|-----------------|--------------|-----------|----------------------------|-------|\n   232→| spikes.times.npy + spikes.clusters.npy + curated cluster list | neural | Bin spike counts in fixed stimulus-aligned time bins for each trial; merge probes within session | code/code_zhang2025 src decoding scripts; IBL loaders | Each trial becomes neuron x time matrix |\n   233→| _ibl_trials.stimOn_times / stimOn_times column | input[0] | Convert to time-since-stimulus-onset representation on a common trial time grid | raw trial table + reference alignment semantics | Because trials are aligned to stimulus onset, this can be represented as the common per-bin relative time values |\n   234→| trial index within block from probabilityLeft runs | input[1] | Continuous per-trial scalar replicated across time bins or stored per trial | raw trial table | Need to compute trial number in current block from probabilityLeft changes |\n   235→| choice column | output[0] | Map left->0, right->1 using IBL choice coding after confirming sign convention | raw trial table | Per-trial categorical output |\n   236→| probabilityLeft column | output[1] | Map 0.2->0, 0.5->1, 0.8->2 | raw trial table | Per-trial categorical output |\n   237→| _ibl_wheel.position.npy + _ibl_wheel.timestamps.npy | output[2] | Differentiate/interpolate to stimulus-aligned bins, convert to speed, discretize into 3 bins | wheel processing in reference workflow / raw wheel arrays | Time-varying categorical output |\n   238→| left/rightCamera.ROIMotionEnergy.npy + camera times | output[3] | Interpolate to stimulus-aligned bins; choose whisker-related ROI motion energy stream and discretize into 3 bins | video motion-energy usage in paper | Time-varying categorical output |\n   239→| clusters.channels.npy + channel/electrode brain-location arrays | brain_region_idx | Map each curated neuron to brain region label/index | probe/channel metadata | Need region-name lookup from available atlas metadata |\n   240→| subject/date/session path | subjects, subject_idx | Extract subject IDs and session ordering | session path structure | One session per converted session entry |\n   241→\n   242→### Key Decisions\n   243→1. **Alignment event**: Align all modalities to stimulus onset because the task explicitly requires this, even though some paper decoders used target-specific alignment.\n   244→2. **Neural representation**: Use binned spike counts rather than rates/dF/F because this is electrophysiology data and the reference code/papers decode from binned spikes.\n   245→3. **Neuron curation**: Apply paper-compatible well-isolated neuron filtering using available cluster metrics before conversion.\n   246→4. **Probe merging**: Merge neurons across probes within a session while preserving per-neuron brain region labels.\n   247→5. **Wheel output**: Derive wheel speed from raw wheel position timestamps, then discretize into 3 bins for categorical decoding as required by the task.\n   248→6. **Whisker output**: Use ROI motion energy as whisker motion energy; if both left and right are available, choose a consistent rule (for example mean or preferred available side) and document it.\n   249→7. **Block-trial input**: Compute trial number within block from consecutive runs of constant probabilityLeft.\n   250→\n   251→### Planned Sanity Checks\n   252→- [ ] Check that trial-table choice and probabilityLeft for a few raw trials exactly match converted outputs.\n   253→- [ ] Check that binned spike counts for a sampled neuron/trial equal counts computed directly from raw spike times with np.allclose().\n   254→- [ ] Check that wheel speed bins come from correctly differentiated/interpolated raw wheel traces for sampled trials.\n   255→- [ ] Check that whisker motion-energy bins match discretization of raw ROI motion-energy traces for sampled trials.\n   256→\n   257→---\n   258→\n   259→## Step 6: Script Development\n   260→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   261→\n   262→[Implementation notes]\n   263→\n   264→Code inefficiencies identified:\n   265→[Note]\n   266→\n   267→Code speedups added:\n   268→[Note]\n   269→\n   270→---\n   271→\n   272→## Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n   273→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   274→\n   275→### Sample Statistics\n   276→| Statistic | Value |\n   277→|-----------|-------|\n   278→| Neurons (total) | 340 |\n   279→| Neurons / session | |\n   280→| Subjects | |\n   281→| Sessions / subject | |\n   282→| Trials (total) | |\n   283→| Trials / session | |\n   284→| <Input statistic 1 range> | [MIN, MAX] |\n   285→| ... | [MIN, MAX] |\n   286→| <Output statistic 1 distribution> | [FRAC0,FRAC1,...] |\n   287→| ... | [FRAC0,FRAC1,...] |\n   288→\n   289→### Processing Plots Review\n   290→Created processing_NYU-11_2020-02-18_001.png and processing_NYU-11_2020-02-21_001.png. decoding uses binned spike counts.\n- Paper states decoding time windows and alignment depend on target variable; for wheel they used 20 ms nonoverlapping bins around first wheel movement.\n- For our conversion task, we must instead align trials to stimulus onset while otherwise matching reference loading/filtering semantics as closely as possible.\n- Probes within the same session and region were combined rather than decoded separately.\n- Wheel speed/velocity in the paper are continuous and decoded with causal windows; our task requires discretized 3-bin categorical wheel speed.\n- Whisker motion energy is computed from left/right whisker-pad ROI motion energy traces from video.\n\n### Curation Steps\n\n**Neuron curation rules**:\n- Exclude units failing any of the three criteria in the paper: amplitude > 50 uV, noise cut-off < 20 uV, and refractory period violation criterion (per RIGOR / ref. 28).\n- Well-isolated neurons are the remaining units.\n- Final analyses further restricted to grey-matter regions with at least 5 well-isolated neurons per session and at least 2 sessions.\n\n**Trial curation rules**:\n- For some paper decoders, additional trial restrictions depended on target variable.\n- Example from visible text: stimulus-side decoding excluded zero-contrast trials.\n- Need to confirm trial inclusion rules relevant to choice / prior / wheel / whisker outputs for our task.\n\n### Decoders Trained\n| Decoded variable | Accuracy |\n| choice | balanced accuracy used in paper; if both left and right are available, choose a consistent rule (for example mean or preferred available side) and document it.\n7. **Block-trial input**: Compute trial number within block from consecutive runs of constant probabilityLeft.\n\n### Planned Sanity Checks\n- [ ] Check that trial-table choice and probabilityLeft for a few raw trials exactly match converted outputs.\n- [ ] Check that binned spike counts for a sampled neuron/trial equal counts computed directly from raw spike times with np.allclose().\n- [ ] Check that wheel speed bins come from correctly differentiated/interpolated raw wheel traces for sampled trials.\n- [ ] Check that whisker motion-energy bins match discretization of raw ROI motion-energy traces for sampled trials.\n\n---\n\n## Step 6: Script Development\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n[Implementation notes]\n\nCode inefficiencies identified:\n[Note]\n\nCode speedups added:\n[Note]\n\n---\n\n## Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Sample Statistics\n| Statistic | Value |\n|-----------|-------|\n| Neurons (total) | 340 |\n| Neurons / session | |\n| Subjects | |\n| Sessions / subject | |\n| Trials (total) | |\n| Trials / session | |\n| <Input statistic 1 range> | [MIN, MAX] |\n| ... | [MIN, MAX] |\n| <Output statistic 1 distribution> | [FRAC0,FRAC1,...] |\n| ... | [FRAC0,FRAC1,...] |\n\n### Processing Plots Review\nCreated processing_NYU-11_2020-02-18_001.png and processing_NYU-11_2020-02-21_001.png. list of sessions\\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\\n\\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\\n\\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'metadata': {\\n        'task_description': str, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\\n        ...\\n    }\\n}\\n```\\n\\n### Key Format Requirements\\n\\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \\n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\\n\\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \\n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \\n\\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. Negative means before event, positive after. \\n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\\n\\n## Python environment\\n\\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\\n\\n## Decoder Reference\\n\\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\\n- Optional arguments:\\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\\n\\n### Purpose\\n\\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\\n1. (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\\n  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `code` directory. \\n- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\\n  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\\n  - Temporal alignment of trials\\n  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\\n  - Brain regions recorded from\\n- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\\n- Check for variables indicating valid data periods \\u2014 exclude invalid data\\n- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\\n- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\\n\\n**Done when**:\\n- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n- You have documented all decisions under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 6: Script Development\\n\\n**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `convert_data.py`.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Write all conversion code in the file `convert_data.py`\\n- Script should run as **`python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\\n- Include options:\\n  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\\n  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\\n  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\\n- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\\n- Write efficient code:\\n  - Vectorize loops\\n  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\\n  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\\n  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\\n- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\\n  - There are no temporal misalignments\\n  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\\n- Write clear, well-documented functions\\n- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\\n- Validate data shapes and types at each step\\n- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\\n\\n**Done when**: `convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u convert_data.py sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee conversion_sample_out.txt`\\n  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\\n  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\\n  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \\n  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\\n2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\\n  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \\n  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \\n  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \\n3. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_sample_out.txt`.\\n4. Analyze the output file `verification_sample_out.txt`:\\n  a. Verify no errors reported.\\n  b. Attempt to address any warnings\\n  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- `sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- Processing plots show no anomalies\\n- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 7\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `sample_data.pkl` and `verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\\n2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\\n\\n**Investigate any issues**:\\n- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- Decoder training completes without errors\\n- Loss decreases over epochs\\n- Accuracies are above chance\\n- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 8\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\\n\\n**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\\n2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\\n3. Run `python -u convert_data.py converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee conversion_full_out.txt`\\n4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \\n5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \\n6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \\n7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\\n8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\\n9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\\n\\n**Done when**:\\n- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\\n- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 9\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 10: Critical Review 1\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\\n\\n**Check 1: Output log verification**\\nRead through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\\n- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\\n- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\\n- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \\n- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \\n- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \\n- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\\n- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \\n- For each major processing step \\u2014 (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction \\u2014 identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \\n- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \\n- Fix any mismatches. \\n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\\n\\n**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\\n- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference papers. \\n- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \\n- Fix any discovered issues. \\n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\\n- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \\n- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\nWrite a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 10\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \\n2. **Wait for complete execution** \\u2014 this may take significant time for large datasets\\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the papers. \\n\\n**Done when**: \\n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 11\\\" with a table of accuracies\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\\n\\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \\n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \\n\\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to papers**\\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference papers\\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the papers's accuracy for each variable\\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \\n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \\n\\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \\n\\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\\n2. Check temporal alignment \\u2014 plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \\n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and papers. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\n**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 12\\\".\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\\n\\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\\n  - Key decisions and rationale\\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\\n  - Validation results and tables\\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\\n- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\\n  - Dataset description\\n  - How to load and use the converted data\\n  - Output format specification\\n  - Key statistics\\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\\n- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\\n\\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\\n\\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. Final\r\nanalyses were additionally restricted to regions that were designated grey matter in the adult mouse Allen Common Coordinate framework6, contained at least five\r\n well-isolated neurons per session and were recorded from in at least two such sessions.\r\n58:We briefly describe the video analysis pipeline (full details can be found in ref. 105). The recording rigs contained three cameras: one called ‘left’ at ful\r\nl resolution (1,280 × 1,024) and 60 Hz, filming the mouse from one side; one called ‘right’, filming the mouse symmetrically from the other side at half resolut\r\nion (640 × 512) and 150 Hz; and one called ‘body’ at half resolution and 30 Hz, filming the body of the mouse from above. We developed several quality-control m\r\netrics to detect raw video issues such as poor illumination (as infrared light bulbs broke) or accidental misplacement of the cameras105.\r\n60:We computed the motion energy (the mean across pixels of the absolute value of the difference between adjacent frames) of the whisker pad areas in the ‘left’\r\n and ‘right’ videos (Fig. 1d). The whisker pad area was empirically defined using a rectangular bounding box anchored between the nose tip and the eye, both fou\r\nnd using DeepLabCut106 (DLC; see more below). This metric quantifies motion in the whisker pad area and has a temporal resolution of the respective camera.\r\n62:We also performed markerless pose estimation of body parts using DLC31, which is used in a fully automated pipeline in IBL (v.2.1) to track various body part\r\ns such as the paws, nose, tongue and pupil (Fig. 1d). In all analyses using DLC estimates, we excluded predictions with likelihood < 0.9. Furthermore, we develo\r\nped several quality-control metrics for the DLC traces105.\r\n66:We performed a decoding analysis to measure how much information the activity of populations of neurons contained about task variables such as stimulus side\r\nand choice. To do this we, used cross-validated, maximum-likelihood regression with L1 regularization (to zero out the contribution from noisy neurons). The neu\r\nral regressors were defined by binning the spike counts from each neuron in each session in a given region in a specific time window on each trial. The duration\r\n of the time window, the number of bins in that time window (that is, the bin size) and the trial event to which it was aligned depended on the variable that is\r\n the target of our regression (Supplementary Table 2). These factors are discussed further below and include a variety of behavioural and task variables: stimul\r\nus side, choice, feedback and wheel speed and velocity. Although a session may have included multiple probe insertions, we did not perform decoding on these pro\r\nbes separately because they are not independent. Instead, neurons in the same session and region were combined across probes for our decoding analysis. Decoding\r\n was cross-validated and compared with a null distribution to test for significance. A given region may have been recorded on multiple sessions; therefore, in t\r\nhe main figures (Figs. 4–7) the region P value was defined by combining session P values using Fisher’s combined probability test, and the region effect size wa\r\ns defined by subtracting the median of the null distribution from the decoding score and reporting the median of the resulting values across sessions. The P val\r\nues for all regions were then subjected to FDR correction for multiple comparisons at q = 0.01.\r\n70:Stimulus side, choice and feedback were treated as binary target variables for logistic regression. For stimulus side, trials that had zero contrast were exc\r\nluded. We used the LogisticRegression module from scikit-learn111 (v.1.1.2) with 0.001 tolerance, 20,000 maximum iterations, “l1” penalty, “liblinear” solver an\r\nd “fit_intercept” set to True. We balanced decoder classes by weighting samples by the inverse of the class frequency, 1/(2Pi,class). Decoding performance was e\r\nvaluated using the balanced accuracy of classification, which is the average of the recall probabilities for the two classes. Supplementary Figure 9 shows histo\r\ngrams of the regression coefficients for all the variables.\r\n72:Wheel values (speed and velocity) change over the course of a trial, unlike the previous decoding targets, and we therefore had to treat these target variabl\r\nes differently. We averaged wheel values in nonoverlapping 20-ms bins, starting 200 ms before first wheel-movement time and ending at 1,000 ms after first wheel\r\n-movement time. Spike counts were similarly binned. The target value for a given bin (ending at time t) was decoded from spikes in a preceding (causal) window s\r\npanning W bins (ending at times t, …, t-W). Therefore, if decoding from n neurons, there were (W + 1)n predictors of the target variable in a given bin. In prac\r\ntice, we used W = 10. To decode these continuous-valued targets, we performed linear regression using the Lasso module from scikit-learn111 (v.1.1.2) with 0.001\r\n tolerance, 1,000 maximum iterations and “fit_intercept” set to True. (Step 28, `find_sessions` function)\n\n**Reasoning**: The agent identified the ONE cache structure during Step 2 data exploration (Step 8 analysis) and confirmed file availability in Steps 10-14.\n\n---\n\n### 2. Data Splitting into Subjects, Sessions, Trials\n\n**Subjects**: Extracted from session path component `session_path.parts[-3]` (the subject name directory in the ONE cache hierarchy). 141 unique subjects found.\n\n**Sessions**: Each unique directory containing `_ibl_trials.table.pqt` constitutes a session. 461 sessions total (460 kept after filtering).\n\n**Trials**: Each row of the Parquet trial table is a trial. 297,505 total trials (mean ~645/session, median 601). After filtering for valid choice ({-1, 1}) and non-NaN `stimOn_times`/`probabilityLeft`, approximately 296,266 trials were retained.\n\n**Reasoning**: The agent computed trial counts by loading parquet files and counting rows (Steps 14, 24). Subject extraction directly from path structure was a straightforward decision.\n\n---\n\n### 3. Trial Filtering (Quality Controls)\n\n**Filters applied**:\n1. **Non-NaN stimulus onset**: `trials['stimOn_times'].notna()`\n2. **Valid choice only**: `trials['choice'].isin([-1, 1])` -- excludes no-go/miss trials coded as 0 in the raw data\n3. **Non-NaN probability left**: `trials['probabilityLeft'].notna()`\n4. **Minimum trial count**: Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials are skipped (`len(trials) < 2` returns None)\n5. **Zero-neural trial removal** (added in Step 2127): Trials where all neural bins are zero are removed: `keep_trial = np.array([np.any(m != 0) for m in neural], dtype=bool)`\n\n**Reasoning**: The agent discovered that raw `choice` values are `{-1, 0, 1}` in Step 39 and recognized that 0 represents no-go/miss trials that are invalid for a binary choice decoder. This was fixed in Step 43. The zero-neural trial filter was added in Step 2127 after full verification revealed many sessions with all-zero neural data warnings.\n\n---\n\n### 4. Neural Data Processing (Spike Binning and Neuron Filtering)\n\n**Neuron filtering criteria** (from `load_curated_spikes`):\n- `noise_cutoff < 20` (from `clusters.metrics.pqt`)\n- `label >= 1` (from `clusters.metrics.pqt`)\n\n**What was NOT implemented**: The reference papers specified three criteria for well-isolated neurons:\n- Amplitude > 50 uV\n- Noise cutoff < 20 uV\n- Refractory period violation threshold per RIGOR\n\nThe agent only implemented `noise_cutoff < 20` and `label >= 1`. The agent noted in Step 21 that \"amplitude > 50 uV\" and \"refractory period violation threshold per RIGOR\" were criteria from the papers, but the actual implementation used `label >= 1` as a proxy (Step 29 analysis: \"provisional curation using available fields; amplitude criterion may require conversion/field interpretation refinement later\"). The `label` field in `clusters.metrics.pqt` was used as a combined quality indicator.\n\n**Spike binning** (`bin_spikes_for_trials`):\n- Window: -0.2s to +1.0s relative to stimulus onset (`t0=-0.2`, `t1=1.0`)\n- Bin size: 20 ms (`bin_size=0.02`)\n- Method: `np.arange(t0, t1 + 1e-9, bin_size)` creates edges; spikes are binned using `np.floor((rel - t0) / bin_size)` and accumulated with `np.add.at(mat, (clu[good], tb[good]), 1)` for spike counts per neuron per time bin\n- Output: `(n_neurons, n_timepoints)` matrix per trial, dtype `float32`\n\n**Multi-probe handling**: Probes within a session are combined. Each probe's curated neurons are concatenated with an offset to produce a single neuron index space per session.\n\n**Brain regions**: Initially used placeholder `channel_*` labels, then patched (Step 38) to use `ccf_<id>` labels from `channels.brainLocationIds_ccf_2017.npy`. No acronym lookup was implemented; the CCF integer IDs were used directly.\n\n---\n\n### 5. Alignment to Stimulus Onset\n\nNeural data, wheel speed, and whisker motion energy are all aligned to `stimOn_times` from the trial table.
